# Basic structure

- bench github contains scripts
  + bench.py to run

- Install and set up benchexec

- tools directory:
  + <tool>.py defines methods etc. that allows benchexec to run the tool.

- suites from benchmark-defs:
  + XML files that define tasks and options for the tool in "rundefinitions".
    Tasks point to .yml files or .set files which lists .yml files.
  + WARNING: The propertyfile in a rundefinition MUST be the one that a YML
    file refers to.

- yml files: fixed format that we should generate from smt or C files
  + WARNING: A YML file specifies properties and their expected verdicts.
    The property file that it refers to MUST BE the same one that is mentioned
    in any task that runs it.
    MORE WARNING: There are two directories of properties, one of which extends
    the other (../tasks/properties/... vs ../properties/..). Make sure you use
    the right one; otherwise, the set of files will be empty.

- Terminology for bench.py

  - tool: This points to benchmark-defs/<tool>.xml, which is the ROOT. It
    contains:

	+ Pointer to the tool, e.g., <tools.inthull>. This points to the python
	  file for the tool, e.g., `IntHull.py` in the `tools` directory.
	  benchexec looks for <tools.inthull> in some default directory or within
	  the current directory (I think).

	+ rundefinitions, whose name is what you specify at the command line for
	  benchexec. See below.

	+ tasks. "suite" in the terminology of `bench.py`.

  - rundefinitions: each rundefinition in <tool>.xml specifies

	+ A list of "options" that are what's fed to the program to run for
	  benchmarking.

	+ A list of tasks. Each task is a "suite" in the terminology of
	  `bench.py`.
	  Each task contains a number of files to run; these files are in
	  tasks/. A `.set` file includes what yml files to run.

	  A yml file holds metadata corresponding to say an SMT file, so they
	  are in 1-1 correspondence. A YML file specifies things like the property
	  to test for and the verdict.

  - suite: task.

# SMT sources

- Better than below:
  `duet.exe -monotone -cra <some-c-file>` triggers `LossyTranslation`, which calls
  convex hull.

- `duet.exe -monotone -cra -cra-vass (or -cra-vas) -dump-hulls <some-c-file>`

- `duet.exe -termination -monotone -no-phase`

The point is to use a domain like `PolyhedronGuard` that calls `Abstract.abstract`
to compute the convex hull. Need to use Zak's old dump-hulls repository!

This dumps the obligations encountered by `abstract` in analyzing the C file.

- Use `generate_hull_obligations.sh` in my dump-hulls git repo to automate this process.

# Preparing SMT files for benchexec

- Unzip SMT folder so that the result looks like `bench/tasks/<folder>/<subfolder>/*.smt2`.
  (Recall that `bench` is the github repository created by Zak.)
- In tasks: `python create_bench_yml.py --folder <folder> --num <number-of-files-to-sample>`.
  This creates yml files that sit beside SMT2 files in each subdirectory.
- Create a `.set` file that lists all YML files.
- Add the set file to a rundefinition and task in `benchmark-defs/<tool>.xml`.
- Run the command line for `bench.py` below.

- How to write a paper:
  - Goal, the problem being solved. Here, convex hull of existentially quantifier
	linear arithmetic formulas.
  - Context: What's known about it, what is the existing work,
	what are the exciting applications. Refer to them! They are all for linear
	real arithmetic, while our work handles both LIA and LIRA.
	Also, for LIA, computation is expensive;
  - Gap:
  - Solution: How did I address it?

- How to find a topic:
  - When you read papers, look at the state of the art. What does that tell you?
	The absence of anything indicates little attention paid to it, or that the
	problem is hard, or that it is not so important. In all these cases,
	there is an opportunity for investigation. E.g. if it's not important, why
	is it not important; how is it instead solved or bypassed?

# TODO (Meeting 11th Jan 2024)

- Computing strongest consequences for uninterpreted functions:
  build up the congruence closure graph, use the model to identify branches
  in the Ackermann reduction/functional consistency axioms. Crucially,
  disequalities in the arguments of functions must be conjoined if two function
  terms are not equal!

- Define abstract domains for each step for each phase of the computation, for
  LIA and LIRA. IFL = polyhedra in floor and original variables, intersected
  with a lattice. L-polyhedra = polyhedra in floor and fractional dimensions,
  intersected with a lattice. Crucially: an L-polyhedron is a not a polyhedron,
  and in particular not the lattice hull; it is the intersection of the
  polyhedron with L, and is e.g., not convex.

- Well-quasiorder doesn't require NNCs, because our abstraction is always a
  closed polyhedron.
